These thresholds adapt ETS practice~\\citep{clauser1998dif} and are portable across populations.           "-Stable-item rank preservation holds across all six benchmarks (mean $\\rho = 0.997$, $\\tau = 0.958$; pairwise flip rate $\\leq 2.8\\%$; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): aggregate ordering is preserved despite substantial measurement instability. Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives ($n = 315$) gain ${+}53$ median rank positions, while Mistral derivatives ($n = 619$) lose $35$ (\\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). That $\\rho \\approx 0.997$ coexists with these local reversals illustrates a general pattern: aggregate stability can mask substantial model-level instability. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. DIF-ordered 一致差于 random (Δρ = -0.001 to -0.003)\n- ρ never drops below 0.985 even at 50% DIF-ordered removal\n- WinoGrande (1,267 items) most sensitive: ρ<0.995 at 25% DIF-ordered; GSM8K most robust: ρ>0.997 at 50%\n- DIF-ordered removal at 20% produces Δρ>0.001 on 4/6 benchmarks\n\n**重写要求**：\n1. 保持 3-tier structure (Monitor/Flag/Act) 但加实证依据\n2. 调整阈值表述：不再是 \"adapt ETS practice\"，而是 \"empirical ρ degradation simulation across six benchmarks shows...\"\n3. 每个 tier 加具体可执行步骤：\n   - Monitor (C% < 15%): ρ degradation negligible across all benchmarks; report C% in evaluation release notes\n   - Flag (15-25%): DIF-ordered removal degrades ρ by 0.001-0.003 more than random; report stable-item rankings as sensitivity analysis alongside full rankings\n   - Act (C% ≥ 25%): multiple benchmarks show ρ < 0.995 under DIF-ordered removal; conduct item content review for flagged items, cross-reference with external contamination indicators, report stable-item rankings as primary comparability metric\n4. 一句话概括：thresholds reflect where DIF-ordered degradation reliably exceeds random removal, not where ρ drops below a fixed cutoff\n5. 保留 DIF pre-screening 和 habba2026growingpains, zhu2026bhi 引用\n6. §5.3 扩写后的目标长度：~8-10 行（当前 5 行）\n\n---\n\n## 改动 2: 页面预算压缩（目标 enlargethispage ≤2）\n\n当前 enlargethispage=6\\baselineskip。§5.3 扩写 +3-5 行，需要从其他地方压 ~7-9 行。\n\n**具体压缩目标**：\n\na) **experiments.tex §4.4** (line 59): 删除 \"The near-perfect θ–total score correlation confirms that the analysis is essentially CTT-based; IRT provides no incremental information for ability stratification in this application.\" — Table 2 Outcome 列已有此信息。SAVE ~2 lines.\n\nb) **related_work.tex §2 DIF paragraph** (line 11): 缩短 \"The scale of this analysis (5,227 examinees across a 57-subject multidimensional ability space) exceeds typical educational testing applications, where \\citet{belzak2020smallsample} note that DIF methods can behave differently at extreme sample sizes.\" → 改为 \"The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications \\citep{belzak2020smallsample}.\" SAVE ~1.5 lines.\n\nc) **related_work.tex §2 DIF paragraph** (line 11): 末尾 \"The primary methodological challenge is the impact-versus-DIF confound: genuine ability differences between temporal cohorts can inflate DIF estimates. We address this through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT θ-conditioning, and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\" → 改为 \"The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT θ-conditioning, and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\" SAVE ~1 line.\n\nd) **experiments.tex §4.6** (line 89): 删除末句 \"MMLU's 12{,}508 items provide sufficient redundancy; even the smallest benchmark (TruthfulQA, 817 items, 574 stable) preserves ρ = 0.997.\" — Table 3 已有此数据。SAVE ~1 line.\n\ne) **discussion.tex §5.2** (line 15): 缩短 \"The semi-synthetic dose-response confirms detection works when differential behavior exists; the disconnect is in the ground truth, not the method.\" → \"Semi-synthetic dose-response confirms detection works when present; the disconnect is in the ground truth.\" SAVE ~0.5 line.\n\n如果仍需更多空间：\nf) **introduction.tex paragraph 2** (line 6): 缩短方法列表 \"from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}\" → \"from membership inference \\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors \\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}\" SAVE ~0.5 line.\n\n---\n\n## 改动 3: limitations.tex §3 \"Matching boundary\" 修复\n\n当前 line 10 说 \"cluster bootstrap at the model-family level is a natural extension\"——但上一段（Examinee non-independence）已经报告了 cluster bootstrap 结果。更新为 \"cluster bootstrap at the model-family level (\\Cref{sec:limitations} above) yields a wider CI partly due to family-cohort confounding\" 或直接删掉多余的句子，避免暗示 cluster bootstrap 尚未做。\n\n---\n\n## 引用验证：不需要\n\n## Commit message: [paper] P34 Batch 3: MF2 ρ degradation in §5.3, page budget compression"

**关键数据（用于 §5.3 文本）**：
- 6 benchmarks 的 ρ degradation simulation：按 |Δ_MH| 降序移除 items vs random removal
- At observed C%≈30%: ρ(DIF-ordered) = 0.994-0.999, mean 0.997; DIF-ordered 一致差于 random (Δρ = -0.001 to -0.003)
- ρ never drops below 0.985 even at 50% DIF-ordered removal
- WinoGrande (1,267 items) most sensitive: ρ<0.995 at 25% DIF-ordered; GSM8K most robust: ρ>0.997 at 50%
- DIF-ordered removal at 20% produces Δρ>0.001 on 4/6 benchmarks

**重写要求**：
1. 保持 3-tier structure (Monitor/Flag/Act) 但加实证依据
2. 调整阈值表述：不再是 "adapt ETS practice"，而是 "empirical ρ degradation simulation across six benchmarks shows..."
3. 每个 tier 加具体可执行步骤：
   - Monitor (C% < 15%): ρ degradation negligible across all benchmarks; report C% in evaluation release notes
   - Flag (15-25%): DIF-ordered removal degrades ρ by 0.001-0.003 more than random; report stable-item rankings as sensitivity analysis alongside full rankings
   - Act (C% ≥ 25%): multiple benchmarks show ρ < 0.995 under DIF-ordered removal; conduct item content review for flagged items, cross-reference with external contamination indicators, report stable-item rankings as primary comparability metric
4. 一句话概括：thresholds reflect where DIF-ordered degradation reliably exceeds random removal, not where ρ drops below a fixed cutoff
5. 保留 DIF pre-screening 和 habba2026growingpains, zhu2026bhi 引用
6. §5.3 扩写后的目标长度：~8-10 行（当前 5 行）

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{Methodological scope.}
4	MH-DIF detects only uniform DIF; logistic regression reveals an additional 24.8\% non-uniform DIF, making 31\% conservative. More fundamentally, DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently but cannot determine \emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts. A definitive causal decomposition remains out of reach without access to model training data. The semi-synthetic framework uses binary response flipping (incorrect $\to$ correct) to simulate differential contamination, representing a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern. Real-world contamination involves partial memorization, reasoning shortcuts, and format-specific advantages; detection power under such realistic patterns is untested. The moderate $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$ reflects sensitivity to severe differential behavior; the framework's primary methodological contribution---cross-subject matching achieving $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$---is a matching strategy property independent of injection realism.
5	
6	\paragraph{Examinee non-independence.}
7	The MH $\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees, yet LLMs sharing base models, training data, or architectures violate this assumption. Empirical mitigations partially address this: permutation simulation bounds LD-attributable FPR inflation to ${<}0.001$~pp, and graduated de-duplication ($K{=}20$: C\% $= 29.1\%$) shows family structure does not inflate results. \citet{french2010hierarchical} show that MH maintains nominal size under moderate clustering in educational settings, consistent with our empirical findings; however, the extreme relatedness of some LLM families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in that work. Cluster bootstrap resampling at the family level (29 families with $\geq$3 members, 1{,}882 models, $B = 1{,}000$) yields a 95\% CI of $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$, $3.1\times$ wider than the model-level CI $[29.4\%, 38.1\%]$, partly because LLM families are cohort-specific (no family spans both 2023 and 2024), so resampling families simultaneously varies the reference/focal composition. Despite this conservative bound, the lower bound far exceeds the random-split baseline (0.18\%), confirming robustness to within-family dependence.
8	
9	\paragraph{Matching boundary and sample size.}
10	Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; when contamination spans ${>}10$ subjects (${>}18\%$ of MMLU), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069. Model-level bootstrap may underestimate CI width due to within-family correlation; cluster bootstrap at the model-family level is a natural extension (the $K{=}20$ de-duplication analysis at C\% $= 29.1\%$ provides a conservative robustness check). The GSM1K correlation analysis ($N = 33$) has limited statistical power and is exploratory.
11	
12	\paragraph{Generalizability.}
13	All six benchmarks use multiple-choice or binary scoring; generalizability to open-ended generation (e.g., AlpacaEval), coding (e.g., HumanEval), or partial-credit benchmarks is untested and would require polytomous DIF methods. Temporal cohorts are defined by calendar year, a coarse grouping; finer-grained definitions (e.g., by training data cutoff or alignment method) might reveal more targeted patterns.
14	
```

</details>

124	| arc | 40% | >50% | >50% | >50% | -0.0012 |
125	| winogrande | 25% | 35% | 50% | >50% | -0.0021 |
126	| gsm8k | >50% | >50% | >50% | >50% | -0.0004 |
127	| hellaswag | >50% | >50% | >50% | >50% | -0.0014 |
128	| mmlu | >50% | >50% | >50% | >50% | -0.0021 |
129	
130	## Audit Threshold Recommendation
131	
132	### Key findings
133	
134	1. **DIF-ordered removal always degrades ρ faster than random removal.** Across all 6 benchmarks and all removal percentages, DIF-ordered ρ < random ρ (Δρ consistently negative). This confirms that high-|Δ_MH| items disproportionately affect model rankings — the bias is not an artifact of reduced item count.
135	
136	2. **Rankings are remarkably robust.** Even removing 50% of items DIF-ordered, ρ never drops below 0.985 on any benchmark. The ρ < 0.99 threshold is crossed only by WinoGrande (at 50%), suggesting leaderboard rankings are structurally stable.
137	
138	3. **Sensitivity varies by benchmark.** WinoGrande is most sensitive (ρ=0.9855 at 50% DIF removal, Δρ=−0.0053 vs random). GSM8K is most robust (ρ=0.9980 at 50%, Δρ=−0.0008). This correlates with item count: fewer items → more sensitivity per removed item.
139	
140	4. **The DIF−Random gap (Δρ) stabilizes around 20–30% removal** for most benchmarks, indicating marginal items beyond the top 20% contribute less bias per item.
141	
142	### Recommended thresholds
143	
144	- **C ≤ 20%**: Safe zone. DIF-ordered ρ ≥ 0.995 on all benchmarks except WinoGrande (0.9955). Practical impact on rankings is minimal.
145	- **20% < C ≤ 35%**: Caution zone. WinoGrande and ARC drop below ρ=0.995. Flip rates reach 2–3%, meaning ~1 in 35–50 model pairs swap order.
146	- **C > 35%**: Concern zone. Multiple benchmarks show ρ < 0.995. ARC and WinoGrande approach ρ < 0.99.
147	
148	**For the paper's audit protocol, we recommend flagging benchmarks where DIF-ordered removal at C=20% produces Δρ > 0.001 relative to random removal.** Five of 6 benchmarks exceed this threshold at 20%:
149	- MMLU: Δρ = −0.0022
150	- WinoGrande: Δρ = −0.0021
151	- HellaSwag: Δρ = −0.0014
152	- ARC: Δρ = −0.0012
153	- GSM8K: Δρ = −0.0004 (below threshold — rankings are robust to DIF items)
154	- TruthfulQA: Δρ = −0.0002 (below threshold)
155

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}
5	A growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training data analysis. Membership inference approaches estimate whether a model has seen specific items during training: Min-K\% \citep{shi2024mink} and Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang2024minkpp} use token-level log-likelihood statistics, while Time Travel \citep{golchin2024timetravel} probes models with temporal cues and \citet{oren2024proving} prove contamination through exchangeability tests. Performance-based detectors identify contamination through statistical anomalies \citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique,deng2024investigating}. \citet{balloccu2024leak} provide a broader taxonomy of leakage detection strategies. These methods share a common validation practice: they verify detection accuracy against web-overlap or n-gram labels \citep{li2024contamination} that classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \emph{differential item functioning} (whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts). This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our ground truth analysis (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). Work on LLM memorization \citep{carlini2022quantifying, tirumala2022memorization} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. \citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.
6	
7	\paragraph{IRT in LLM Evaluation.}
8	IRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated: \citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT to NLP, \citet{vania2021irt} assessed test set discriminability, and \citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} demonstrated that not all evaluation examples are equally informative. Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \citep{hardy2026scalability}, ATLAS \citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \citep{luo2025medirt}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \citep{habba2026growingpains}, which addresses score comparability through fixed-parameter calibration \citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. Our work is complementary: FPC methods assume a subset of anchor items with stable measurement properties; temporal DIF analysis directly tests this stability assumption and can identify candidate anchor items through DIF pre-screening, providing input to equating procedures~\citep{kolen2014equating}. FPC assumes a stable set of anchor items; our finding that 31\% of items exhibit temporal DIF directly informs anchor selection by identifying which items should \emph{not} serve as anchors. Empirical comparison awaits public release of anchor item lists.
9	
10	\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}
11	The Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\chi^2$ test \citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \citep{raju1988area}; \citet{zumbo1999handbook} provides a comprehensive comparison. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The scale of this analysis (5,227 examinees across a 57-subject multidimensional ability space) exceeds typical educational testing applications, where \citet{belzak2020smallsample} note that DIF methods can behave differently at extreme sample sizes. The primary methodological challenge is the impact-versus-DIF confound: genuine ability differences between temporal cohorts can inflate DIF estimates. We address this through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\theta$-conditioning, and a random-split control (\Cref{sec:temporal}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}
14	The Benchmark Health Index~\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\citep{qian2026benchmark2} assess static benchmark quality; our temporal DIF analysis is complementary, diagnosing \emph{when} measurement properties shift rather than evaluating them at a single snapshot. \citet{maeda2025difllm} predict which item features associate with DIF in educational settings; we instead detect DIF empirically across LLM cohorts at scale. Dynamic benchmarks \citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Chatbot Arena \citep{chiang2024chatbot} sidesteps static benchmarks through pairwise preference. Broader surveys \citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and lack of item-level transparency as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses.
15	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	\enlargethispage{6\baselineskip}
4	
5	\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
6	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	Temporal DIF decomposes benchmark differences into three layers: (1)~\emph{impact}---ability differences removed by matching ($\sim$2.4~pp); (2)~\emph{DIF}---item-level differences after ability control ($\sim$28--29\% floor, 92.4\% item-specific); (3)~\emph{attribution}---contamination, capability evolution, or methodology shifts, underdetermined without training data. Regardless of attribution, temporal DIF means the same score carries different meaning across model generations.
10	
11	\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}
12	\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}
13	
14	\looseness=-1
15	The near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}) reflects a category error: web-overlap measures global exposure, while DIF measures differential functioning, making overlap-based labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. The semi-synthetic dose-response confirms detection works when differential behavior exists; the disconnect is in the ground truth, not the method.
16	
17	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
18	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
19	
20	\looseness=-1
21	We propose running temporal MH-DIF whenever $\geq 500$ new models accumulate ($N \geq 80$ suffices per \Cref{sec:validation}; 500 ensures coverage of diverse model families), with graduated action levels: (1)~\emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$); (2)~\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\emph{Act} (C\% $\geq 25\%$): investigate flagged items and report stable-item rankings, per MMLU-Pro~\citep{wang2024mmlupro} and MMLU-Redux~\citep{gema2024mmlu}. These thresholds adapt ETS practice~\citep{clauser1998dif} and are portable across populations. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}; temporal C\% complements composite quality indices~\citep{zhu2026bhi}.
22	
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
5	
6	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
7	\label{sec:validation}
8	
9	\looseness=-1
10	As approximate parametric bounds, a Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications per condition) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\bar{b} = -0.84$; 14,042 MMLU items) confirms that at $N \geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$ (details in \Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold. Power varies substantially with item discrimination: TPR ranges from 0.40 at $a = 0.5$ to 0.95 at $a = 2.0$; the gap between TPR at mean $a$ (0.87) and the aggregate TPR (0.68) quantifies the power cost of parameter heterogeneity, consistent with Jensen's inequality (\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).
11	
12	\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\% Measurement Instability}
13	\label{sec:temporal}
14	
15	\looseness=-1
16	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; 95\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\%, 38.1\%], $B = 1{,}000$ model-level resamples; rightward asymmetry reflects heavy-tailed C\% from subjects exceeding 40\%). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\% $\to$ 31.30\%), because the dual ETS threshold already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.
17	
18	\looseness=-1
19	Three controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps~\citep{holland1988mh}. The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ with 76.4\% distributional overlap. Under 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$, confirming at most $\sim$2.4~pp is attributable to ability mismatch. Permutation tests confirm ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
20	
21	\looseness=-1
22	Across all 57 subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\%). Item-level regression shows 92.4\% of the DIF signal is item-specific (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$). A Simpson's paradox confirms that DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate accuracy gains: domain accuracy positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level ($\mathrm{OR} = 1.55$, $p = 0.001$) but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\mathrm{OR} = 0.73$, $p = 0.030$). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($\rho = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while showing relative erosion on easy ones. The conclusion is robust across thresholds ($18.3\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$).
23	
24	\begin{table}[t]
25	\centering
26	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. FPR is the flagging rate on web-overlap ``clean'' items~\citep{li2024contamination}, not a traditional false positive rate. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes indicate comparisons where Li et al.\ labels are not applicable.}
27	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}

29	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
30	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}

32	Cohort Definition & $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\
33	\midrule
34	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\
35	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,600 / ${\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
36	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
37	Random split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\
38	Within-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\
39	\bottomrule

41	\end{table}
42	
43	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
44	\label{sec:groundtruth}
45	
46	\looseness=-1
47	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\phi = 0.012$, $\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method. 
48	\begin{figure}[t]
49	\centering
50	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
51	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
52	\label{fig:dose_response}
53	\end{figure}
54	
55	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
56	\label{sec:structural}
57	
58	\looseness=-1
59	Three structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$), and within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$; range 31.1--45.2\% across 5 representative subjects). The elevated within-subject rate partly reflects local dependence: 11.3\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$ vs.\ 2.2\% cross-subject, confirming that cross-subject C\% $= 31.3\%$ is the more conservative estimate. The near-perfect $\theta$--total score correlation confirms that the analysis is essentially CTT-based; IRT provides no incremental information for ability stratification in this application. Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\%), making 31\% conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\%. Ability-matched analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3 C\% from 61.3\% to $28.3\%$ and temporal C\% from 31.3\% to $28.9\%$---consistent matched values ($\sim$28--29\%) despite vastly different unmatched rates, suggesting genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\sim$28--29\% of items. Six alternative explanations were tested; none reduces C\% below $\sim$28\% (\Cref{app:structural}).
60	
61	\begin{table}[t]
62	\centering
63	\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. None reduces temporal C\% below 31\%, indicating that the observed instability is not an artifact of ability matching.}
64	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
65	\small
66	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
67	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
68	\toprule
69	Approach & Mechanism & Temp.\ C\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
70	\midrule
71	Baseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\ total score & $0.314_{\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\pm.027}$ & Reference \\
72	Matched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\ DIF & ${\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\ 0.94--1.04 \\
73	$\theta$-cond. & $\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\theta,\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\
74	Within-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\% $= 38.1\%$ ($>$25\%) \\
75	\bottomrule
76	\end{tabular}}
77	\end{table}
78	
79	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}
80	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
81	
82	\looseness=-1
83	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$, upper-bound estimate under uniform binary flipping; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. Multi-subject injection ($k \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 20\}$ subjects, 5 random draws each) confirms robustness: $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$ for $k \leq 5$ ($\leq$9\% of domains); at $k = 10$ (18\%), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069, marking the boundary of matching variable integrity (\Cref{app:method_details,app:fig_matching}).
84	
85	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
86	\label{sec:downstream}
87	
88	\looseness=-1
89	Stable-item rank preservation holds across all six benchmarks (mean $\rho = 0.997$, $\tau = 0.958$; pairwise flip rate $\leq 2.8\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): aggregate ordering is preserved despite substantial measurement instability. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\ random removal; circularity ablation in \Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives ($n = 315$) gain ${+}53$ median rank positions, while Mistral derivatives ($n = 619$) lose $35$ (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Among the 200 most displaced models, 33.6\% of pairwise rankings reverse---for instance, Qwen1.5-14B-Chat ranks 610 positions ahead of mistral-7b-dpo-v6 on all items but 501 positions behind on stable items alone. Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ larger than random removal; $p < 0.001$), confirming DIF items carry concentrated inter-generational signal. That $\rho \approx 0.997$ coexists with these local reversals illustrates a general pattern: aggregate stability can mask substantial model-level instability. MMLU's 12{,}508 items provide sufficient redundancy; even the smallest benchmark (TruthfulQA, 817 items, 574 stable) preserves $\rho = 0.997$.
90	
91	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
92	\label{sec:gsm8k}
93	
94	\looseness=-1
95	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\times$ above random baselines. Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks (WinoGrande~\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ versus $17.9$--$18.9\%$ for procedural ones, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to training data composition shifts.
96

C\% = ETS~C items ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts; $\rho$/$\tau$ = Spearman/Kendall correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip = pairwise rank-flip rate. All six show substantial DIF (45--174$\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\% than procedural ones.}
100	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}

116	
117	On GSM8K, semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{app:gsm8k_details}); an exploratory GSM1K analysis suggests DIF captures capability change distinct from memorization ($r = -0.478$, 95\% CI $[-0.71, -0.16]$, $p = 0.005$; \Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}). DIF remains substantial even within a 6-month window (2023-H2 vs.\ 2024-H1: MMLU C\% $= 34.35\%$, TruthfulQA $= 31.33\%$).

All six show substantial DIF (45--174$\\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\\% than procedural ones.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & Flip\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.18 & 174$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nOn GSM8K, semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details}); an exploratory GSM1K analysis suggests DIF captures capability change distinct from memorization ($r = -0.478$, 95\\% CI $[-0.71, -0.16]$, $p = 0.005$; \\Cref{app:fig_gsm1k}). DIF remains substantial even within a 6-month window (2023-H2 vs.\\ 2024-H1: MMLU C\\% $= 34.35\\%$, TruthfulQA $= 31.33\\%$).\n",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\% of MMLU~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items. This pattern persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\% range 21--48\%, unweighted mean $\approx 30\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\% of the DIF signal is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this measurement instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\rho \approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.
5	
6	A growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\%~\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}
11	\caption{Global vs.\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}
12	\label{fig:global_vs_differential}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	We apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the approximately 31\% temporal DIF rate cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{sec:gsm8k}). MH-DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts.
16	
17	MMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\rho \approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter: DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\ random removal), and model families show distinctive DIF profiles (Llama-3 gains ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$).
18	
19	Our contributions are methodological and empirical, not algorithmic:
20	
21	\begin{enumerate}
22	\item \textbf{Ground truth meta-critique}: Web-overlap contamination labels~\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).
23	
24	\item \textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework}: Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).
25	
26	\item \textbf{Temporal measurement instability at scale}: Approximately 31\% of MMLU items exhibit differential functioning across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$ (45--174$\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher DIF rates than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
```

Now I have all the data and current file states. Let me plan the edits:

1. **Compressions (a-f)** — apply first to free space
2. **§5.3 rewrite** — with MF2 empirical data
3. **Limitations §3 fix** — remove "natural extension" since cluster bootstrap is already done
4. **§5.2 compression** (e)
5. **Adjust \enlargethispage** from 6 to ≤2

Let me note the SF1 cluster bootstrap text in limitations.tex line 7 — it looks like a hook already expanded it significantly from what I wrote. 

The current limitations text about LLM families and resampling is more comprehensive than my original version, which is fine. Now I'll execute the edits in parallel: removing the near-perfect correlation sentence from §4.4, and condensing the Belzak reference in the related work DIF paragraph. I'm working through these edits systematically. For §4.4, I'll remove that sentence about CTT-based analysis. The related work compressions target line 11 to tighten the impact discussion. I'm deleting the MMLU redundancy claim from §4.6 since even smaller benchmarks maintain high correlation. The dose-response sentence in §5.2 needs condensing, and I should trim the method list in the introduction if it's too verbose. For §5.3, I'm rewriting with the MF2 data showing how DIF-ordered removal consistently degrades correlation faster than random removal—WinoGrande drops below 0.995 at 25% while GSM8K stays above 0.997 even at 50%. Finally, I'll fix the "natural extension" phrasing in the limitations section. I'm establishing the operational framework: we'll run the temporal MH-DIF analysis whenever we accumulate at least 500 new models, grounded in empirical validation showing that 80 models suffice for coverage. The graduated response tiers are calibrated from simulations across six benchmarks comparing DIF-ordered versus random item removal—at under 15% contamination we see negligible degradation, between 15-25% we observe 0.001-0.003 additional loss compared to random removal on most benchmarks, and at 25% or higher we trigger active intervention.

For the flagged and action tiers, I'm reporting stable-item rankings as a sensitivity analysis alongside the full rankings, and when contamination is severe, conducting content review of flagged items while cross-referencing external contamination signals. The thresholds are designed to detect where DIF-ordered removal reliably outpaces random removal rather than relying on fixed correlation cutoffs, which positions DIF pre-screening as a tool for identifying stable anchor items in equating while temporal contamination percentage complements broader quality indices.

Now for the limitations section, I need to acknowledge that cluster bootstrap at the family level has already been addressed in the examinee non-independence discussion, so I'm updating this to note that family-level cluster bootstrap produces wider confidence intervals partly due to cohort-specific family structure, rather than treating it as a future extension. I'm finishing that sentence about the wider confidence interval from the family-level cluster bootstrap, then I'll go ahead and apply all the edits I've identified.
```

    "oldString": "The scale of this analysis (5,227 examinees across a 57-subject multidimensional ability space) exceeds typical educational testing applications, where \\citet{belzak2020smallsample} note that DIF methods can behave differently at extreme sample sizes. The primary methodological challenge is the impact-versus-DIF confound: genuine ability differences between temporal cohorts can inflate DIF estimates. We address this through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).",
    "newString": "The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}\nA growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training data analysis. Membership inference approaches estimate whether a model has seen specific items during training: Min-K\\% \\citep{shi2024mink} and Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang2024minkpp} use token-level log-likelihood statistics, while Time Travel \\citep{golchin2024timetravel} probes models with temporal cues and \\citet{oren2024proving} prove contamination through exchangeability tests. Performance-based detectors identify contamination through statistical anomalies \\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique,deng2024investigating}. \\citet{balloccu2024leak} provide a broader taxonomy of leakage detection strategies. These methods share a common validation practice: they verify detection accuracy against web-overlap or n-gram labels \\citep{li2024contamination} that classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \\emph{differential item functioning} (whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts). This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our ground truth analysis (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). Work on LLM memorization \\citep{carlini2022quantifying, tirumala2022memorization} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. \\citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT in LLM Evaluation.}\nIRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated: \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT to NLP, \\citet{vania2021irt} assessed test set discriminability, and \\citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} demonstrated that not all evaluation examples are equally informative. Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \\citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \\citep{hardy2026scalability}, ATLAS \\citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \\citep{luo2025medirt}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which addresses score comparability through fixed-parameter calibration \\citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. Our work is complementary: FPC methods assume a subset of anchor items with stable measurement properties; temporal DIF analysis directly tests this stability assumption and can identify candidate anchor items through DIF pre-screening, providing input to equating procedures~\\citep{kolen2014equating}. FPC assumes a stable set of anchor items; our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF directly informs anchor selection by identifying which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. Empirical comparison awaits public release of anchor item lists.\n\n\\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{zumbo1999handbook} provides a comprehensive comparison. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The scale of this analysis (5,227 examinees across a 57-subject multidimensional ability space) exceeds typical educational testing applications, where \\citet{belzak2020smallsample} note that DIF methods can behave differently at extreme sample sizes. The primary methodological challenge is the impact-versus-DIF confound: genuine ability differences between temporal cohorts can inflate DIF estimates. We address this through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}\nThe Benchmark Health Index~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\\citep{qian2026benchmark2} assess static benchmark quality; our temporal DIF analysis is complementary, diagnosing \\emph{when} measurement properties shift rather than evaluating them at a single snapshot. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} predict which item features associate with DIF in educational settings; we instead detect DIF empirically across LLM cohorts at scale. Dynamic benchmarks \\citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Chatbot Arena \\citep{chiang2024chatbot} sidesteps static benchmarks through pairwise preference. Broader surveys \\citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and lack of item-level transparency as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 8,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 8,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " IRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated: \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT to NLP, \\citet{vania2021irt} assessed test set discriminability, and \\citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} demonstrated that not all evaluation examples are equally informative. Recent extensions include PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2026psnirt} for benchmark quality at scale, TinyBenchmarks \\citep{maiapolo2024tinybenchmarks} for IRT-based item selection, scalability coefficients for detecting bad benchmark items \\citep{hardy2026scalability}, ATLAS \\citep{li2025atlas} for computerized adaptive testing of LLMs, and competency-focused IRT evaluation in medical domains \\citep{luo2025medirt}. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which addresses score comparability through fixed-parameter calibration \\citep[FPC;][]{kim2019fpc}. Our work is complementary: FPC methods assume a subset of anchor items with stable measurement properties; temporal DIF analysis directly tests this stability assumption and can identify candidate anchor items through DIF pre-screening, providing input to equating procedures~\\citep{kolen2014equating}. FPC assumes a stable set of anchor items; our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF directly informs anchor selection by identifying which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. Empirical comparison awaits public release of anchor item lists.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{DIF Methodology and Measurement Invariance.}",
          "-The Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{zumbo1999handbook} provides a comprehensive comparison. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The scale of this analysis (5,227 examinees across a 57-subject multidimensional ability space) exceeds typical educational testing applications, where \\citet{belzak2020smallsample} note that DIF methods can behave differently at extreme sample sizes. The primary methodological challenge is the impact-versus-DIF confound: genuine ability differences between temporal cohorts can inflate DIF estimates. We address this through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).",
          "+The Mantel-Haenszel procedure for DIF detection was introduced by \\citet{holland1988mh} and remains the most widely used method in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties \\citep{clauser1998dif}. Alternative approaches include logistic regression DIF \\citep{swaminathan1990logistic}, SIBTEST \\citep{shealy1993sibtest}, Lord's $\\chi^2$ test \\citep{lord1980irt}, and the Raju area method \\citep{raju1988area}; \\citet{zumbo1999handbook} provides a comprehensive comparison. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance, vandenberg2000invariance}, which examines whether a measurement instrument functions equivalently across populations. MIMIC models offer a latent-variable approach to the same problem \\citep{muthen1985mimic, woods2009mimic}, and \\citet{teresi2007dif} reviews DIF methods across applied domains. Cross-subject matching---computing the stratification variable from domains other than the one being tested---has psychometric precedent in the DIF literature \\citep{nandakumar1993dif,donoghue1993mh}; our adaptation addresses LLM-specific challenges of multi-domain local dependence and simultaneous contamination control at scale. We do not modify the MH procedure; our contribution is the application context and the empirical findings it reveals. The 5,227-examinee scale exceeds typical DIF applications~\\citep{belzak2020smallsample}. The impact-versus-DIF confound is addressed through matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and a random-split control (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}",
          " The Benchmark Health Index~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} and Benchmark$^2$~\\citep{qian2026benchmark2} assess static benchmark quality; our temporal DIF analysis is complementary, diagnosing \\emph{when} measurement properties shift rather than evaluating them at a single snapshot. \\citet{maeda2025difllm} predict which item features associate with DIF in educational settings; we instead detect DIF empirically across LLM cohorts at scale. Dynamic benchmarks \\citep{kiela2021dynabench, li2024latesteval, white2025livebench} mitigate contamination by construction, yet none provides psychometric equating across versions. Chatbot Arena \\citep{chiang2024chatbot} sidesteps static benchmarks through pairwise preference. Broader surveys \\citep{chang2024survey, burnell2023rethink} identify benchmark saturation and lack of item-level transparency as systemic challenges that our DIF analysis directly addresses."
        ]
      }
    ],

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological scope.}\nMH-DIF detects only uniform DIF; logistic regression reveals an additional 24.8\\% non-uniform DIF, making 31\\% conservative. More fundamentally, DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently but cannot determine \\emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts. A definitive causal decomposition remains out of reach without access to model training data. The semi-synthetic framework uses binary response flipping (incorrect $\\to$ correct) to simulate differential contamination, representing a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern. Real-world contamination involves partial memorization, reasoning shortcuts, and format-specific advantages; detection power under such realistic patterns is untested. The moderate $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$ reflects sensitivity to severe differential behavior; the framework's primary methodological contribution---cross-subject matching achieving $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$---is a matching strategy property independent of injection realism.\n\n\\paragraph{Examinee non-independence.}\nThe MH $\\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees, yet LLMs sharing base models, training data, or architectures violate this assumption. Empirical mitigations partially address this: permutation simulation bounds LD-attributable FPR inflation to ${<}0.001$~pp, and graduated de-duplication ($K{=}20$: C\\% $= 29.1\\%$) shows family structure does not inflate results. \\citet{french2010hierarchical} show that MH maintains nominal size under moderate clustering in educational settings, consistent with our empirical findings; however, the extreme relatedness of some LLM families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in that work. Cluster bootstrap resampling at the family level (29 families with $\\geq$3 members, 1{,}882 models, $B = 1{,}000$) yields a 95\\% CI of $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$, $3.1\\times$ wider than the model-level CI $[29.4\\%, 38.1\\%]$, partly because LLM families are cohort-specific (no family spans both 2023 and 2024), so resampling families simultaneously varies the reference/focal composition. Despite this conservative bound, the lower bound far exceeds the random-split baseline (0.18\\%), confirming robustness to within-family dependence.\n\n\\paragraph{Matching boundary and sample size.}\nCross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; when contamination spans ${>}10$ subjects (${>}18\\%$ of MMLU), $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069. Model-level bootstrap may underestimate CI width due to within-family correlation; cluster bootstrap at the model-family level is a natural extension (the $K{=}20$ de-duplication analysis at C\\% $= 29.1\\%$ provides a conservative robustness check).     "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\enlargethispage{6\\baselineskip}\n\n\\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTemporal DIF decomposes benchmark differences into three layers: (1)~\\emph{impact}---ability differences removed by matching ($\\sim$2.4~pp); (2)~\\emph{DIF}---item-level differences after ability control ($\\sim$28--29\\% floor, 92.4\\% item-specific); (3)~\\emph{attribution}---contamination, capability evolution, or methodology shifts, underdetermined without training data. Regardless of attribution, temporal DIF means the same score carries different meaning across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}) reflects a category error: web-overlap measures global exposure, while DIF measures differential functioning, making overlap-based labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. The semi-synthetic dose-response confirms detection works when differential behavior exists; the disconnect is in the ground truth, not the method.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe propose running temporal MH-DIF whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate ($N \\geq 80$ suffices per \\Cref{sec:validation}; 500 ensures coverage of diverse model families), with graduated action levels: (1)~\\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$); (2)~\\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\\%$): report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings; (3)~\\emph{Act} (C\\% $\\geq 25\\%$): investigate flagged items and report stable-item rankings, per MMLU-Pro~\\citep{wang2024mmlupro} and MMLU-Redux~\\citep{gema2024mmlu}. These thresholds adapt ETS practice~\\citep{clauser1998dif} and are portable across populations. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}; temporal C\\% complements composite quality indices~\\citep{zhu2026bhi}.\n",

    "newString": "from membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. This pattern persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of the DIF signal is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this measurement instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference approaches such as Min-K\\%~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors such as ConStat~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, DVD~\\citep{liang2026dvd}, and Self-Critique~\\citep{tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the approximately 31\\% temporal DIF rate cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}). MH-DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts.\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter: DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal), and model families show distinctive DIF profiles (Llama-3 gains ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$).\n\nOur contributions are methodological and empirical, not algorithmic:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique}: Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework}: Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal measurement instability at scale}: Approximately 31\\% of MMLU items exhibit differential functioning across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$ (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher DIF rates than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

These thresholds adapt ETS practice~\\citep{clauser1998dif} and are portable across populations.   "new_string": "\\looseness=-1\nWe propose running temporal MH-DIF whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate ($N \\geq 80$ suffices per \\Cref{sec:validation}; 500 ensures coverage of diverse model families). Empirical $\\rho$ degradation simulation across six benchmarks---removing items by descending $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$ versus random removal---grounds the following graduated thresholds:\n(1)~\\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$): DIF-ordered $\\rho$ degradation is negligible ($\\Delta\\rho < 0.001$ vs.\\ random on all benchmarks); report C\\% in evaluation release notes.\n(2)~\\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\\%$): DIF-ordered removal degrades $\\rho$ by $0.001$--$0.003$ more than random on 4/6 benchmarks; report stable-item rankings as sensitivity analysis alongside full rankings.\n(3)~\\emph{Act} (C\\% $\\geq 25\\%$): WinoGrande drops below $\\rho = 0.995$ at 25\\% DIF-ordered removal; conduct item content review for flagged items, cross-reference with external contamination indicators, and report stable-item rankings as the primary comparability metric.\nThresholds reflect where DIF-ordered degradation reliably exceeds random removal, not where $\\rho$ drops below a fixed cutoff. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}; temporal C\\% complements composite quality indices~\\citep{zhu2026bhi}."
}

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	\enlargethispage{2\baselineskip}
4	
5	\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
6	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	Temporal DIF decomposes benchmark differences into three layers: (1)~\emph{impact}---ability differences removed by matching ($\sim$2.4~pp); (2)~\emph{DIF}---item-level differences after ability control ($\sim$28--29\% floor, 92.4\% item-specific); (3)~\emph{attribution}---contamination, capability evolution, or methodology shifts, underdetermined without training data. Regardless of attribution, temporal DIF means the same score carries different meaning across model generations.
10	
11	\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}
12	\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}
13	
14	\looseness=-1
15	The near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}) reflects a category error: web-overlap measures global exposure, while DIF measures differential functioning, making overlap-based labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. Semi-synthetic dose-response confirms detection works when present; the disconnect is in the ground truth.
16	
17	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
18	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
19	
20	\looseness=-1
21	We propose running temporal MH-DIF whenever $\geq 500$ new models accumulate ($N \geq 80$ suffices per \Cref{sec:validation}; 500 ensures coverage of diverse model families). Empirical $\rho$ degradation simulation across six benchmarks---removing items by descending $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}|$ versus random removal---grounds the following graduated thresholds:
22	(1)~\emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$): DIF-ordered $\rho$ degradation is negligible ($\Delta\rho < 0.001$ vs.\ random on all benchmarks); report C\% in evaluation release notes.
23	(2)~\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\%$): DIF-ordered removal degrades $\rho$ by $0.001$--$0.003$ more than random on 4/6 benchmarks; report stable-item rankings as sensitivity analysis alongside full rankings.
24	(3)~\emph{Act} (C\% $\geq 25\%$): WinoGrande drops below $\rho = 0.995$ at 25\% DIF-ordered removal; conduct item content review for flagged items, cross-reference with external contamination indicators, and report stable-item rankings as the primary comparability metric.
25	Thresholds reflect where DIF-ordered degradation reliably exceeds random removal, not where $\rho$ drops below a fixed cutoff. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}; temporal C\% complements composite quality indices~\citep{zhu2026bhi}.
26

Regardless of attribution, temporal DIF means the same score carries different meaning across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}) reflects a category error: web-overlap measures global exposure, while DIF measures differential functioning, making overlap-based labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. Semi-synthetic dose-response confirms detection works when present; the disconnect is in the ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe propose running temporal MH-DIF whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate ($N \\geq 80$ suffices per \\Cref{sec:validation}; 500 ensures coverage of diverse model families).
