[Director 指令] [Patch] P35: Round 11 fixes — page overflow + 13 edits

**硬约束**: Conclusion 必须在 page 8 内结束。当前溢出到 page 9。每做 2-3 项修改后编译一次检查 page count。

**Priority A: 页面关键修改（先做这些，编译确认 Conclusion 回到 page 8）**

### A1. §5.3 原位压缩（discussion.tex）— 目标 -3 行
当前 §5.3 约 8 行。压缩为 ~5 行：
- 删 "DIF-ordered versus random item removal across six benchmarks" （§4 已覆盖）
- 每个 tier 删 Δρ 具体数字（保留 C% 阈值 + 行动步骤）
- Act 层删 "cross-reference with contamination indicators"，改为 "review flagged items for content characteristics that may explain differential performance (e.g., surface-level cues, memorization-prone formats)"
- 末句 "DIF pre-screening..." 压缩为半句，合并到前一句

压缩后参考格式：
```
We propose running temporal MH-DIF whenever $\geq 500$ new models accumulate ($N \geq 80$ suffices per \Cref{sec:validation}; 500 ensures family diversity), with graduated action levels grounded in empirical $\rho$ degradation simulation:
(1)~\emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$): report C\% in evaluation release notes.
(2)~\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\%$): report stable-item rankings as sensitivity analysis alongside full rankings.
(3)~\emph{Act} (C\% $\geq 25\%$): review flagged items for content characteristics that may explain differential performance (e.g., surface-level cues, memorization-prone formats); report stable-item rankings as the primary comparability metric.
These thresholds reflect where DIF-ordered $\rho$ degradation reliably exceeds random removal; DIF pre-screening also identifies stable anchor items for equating~\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}.
```

### A2. Contribution 3 压缩（introduction.tex）— 目标 -2 行
当前 item 3 约 6 行。压到 3-4 行：删除 discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$) 和 difficulty--direction 细节（正文 §4 已有）。保留：31% temporal DIF + 6-benchmark replication + C% range + knowledge vs procedural。

### A3. 编译验证
做完 A1+A2 后立即编译，确认 §6 Conclusion 回到 page 8。如果还溢出，进一步压缩 §5.2（删半句）或 introduction paragraph 3。

---

**Priority B: Should-Fix 修改**

### B1. §1 Contribution 2 加限定（introduction.tex）
"achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$" → "achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ under single-subject contamination"

### B2. Abstract 剪枝（abstract.tex）— 减少数字密度，不改结构
删除以下次要指标：
- 删 "$\phi = 0.012$"（enrichment ≈ 0.96 已表达同一信息）
- 删 "$\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.01$ for $k \leq 5$ subjects"（正文有，abstract 保留 ΔFPR=0 即可）
- 删 "45–174$\times$ above random baselines"（保留 C% range 已足够）
保持结构和核心数字不变。

### B3. Table 1 术语修改（experiments.tex）
"FPR (clean)" 列名 → "C\% (web-clean)"
Table 1 caption 中对应的解释也改：删 "not a traditional false positive rate"，改为 "DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \citet{li2024contamination}"

### B4. §4.2 加 family-level CI（experiments.tex）
在 model-level bootstrap CI 句子后加：
"Family-level cluster bootstrap (29 families with $\geq$3 members) yields a wider CI of [26.7\%, 53.2\%], $3.1\times$ the model-level width, confirming robustness under within-family dependence (details in Limitations)."

### B5. §3.2 LLM-specific adaptation（method.tex）
在 "LLM-specific challenges" 段末尾加一句：
"Unlike standard purified MH that conditions on total test score, we match on cross-subject total score to avoid criterion contamination when target-subject items themselves may be compromised---an adaptation specific to multi-domain LLM benchmarks where subjects may be differentially affected."

### B6. §4.4 De-duplication framing（experiments.tex）
"confirms family structure does not inflate C\%" → "reduces C\% moderately (confounded with statistical power reduction from smaller $N$); even $K{=}10$ (C\% $= 22.7\%$) exceeds the random-split baseline by ${>}100\times$"

---

**Priority C: 润色修改**

### C1. §4.6 Selection bias（experiments.tex）
"Among the 200 most displaced models" → "Among the 200 most displaced models (top 3.8\% by $|\Delta\mathrm{rank}|$; full-sample pairwise flip rate = 2.03\%)"

### C2. §4.7 GSM1K wording（experiments.tex）
如果有 "suggests"，改为 "is consistent with"

### C3. Limitations 加 closed-source caveat（limitations.tex）
在 Generalizability 段末尾加：
"All models are drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard; generalizability to closed-source models cannot be assessed."

### C4. Table 3 caption（experiments.tex）
检查 Ratio 列，如果 caption 没有说明 baseline 依赖性，加："Ratio = C\%/baseline C\%; baseline varies by benchmark."

### C5. Missing references（references.bib）
新建 bib 条目（如不存在）：
- Penfield & Camilli (2007): "Differential Item Functioning" in *Handbook of Statistics* Vol. 26
- Millsap (2011): *Statistical Approaches to Measurement Invariance*, Routledge

在 related_work.tex DIF 段或 method.tex 已有引用列表中加 \cite{}，不加新散文文本。

1. 编译通过，0 critical warnings
2. §6 Conclusion 完全在 page 8 内
3. \enlargethispage 仍为 2（不增加）

```text
19	Our contributions are methodological and empirical, not algorithmic:
20	
21	\begin{enumerate}
22	\item \textbf{Ground truth meta-critique}: Web-overlap contamination labels~\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).
23	
24	\item \textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework}: Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).
25	
26	\item \textbf{Temporal measurement instability at scale}: Approximately 31\% of MMLU items exhibit differential functioning across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\%$ (45--174$\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher DIF rates than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).
27	\end{enumerate}
28	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	\enlargethispage{2\baselineskip}
4	
5	\subsection{Implications for Benchmark Temporal Comparability}
6	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
7	
8	\looseness=-1
9	Temporal DIF decomposes benchmark differences into three layers: (1)~\emph{impact}---ability differences removed by matching ($\sim$2.4~pp); (2)~\emph{DIF}---item-level differences after ability control ($\sim$28--29\% floor, 92.4\% item-specific); (3)~\emph{attribution}---contamination, capability evolution, or methodology shifts, underdetermined without training data. Regardless of attribution, temporal DIF means the same score carries different meaning across model generations.
10	
11	\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}
12	\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}
13	
14	\looseness=-1
15	The near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}) reflects a category error: web-overlap measures global exposure, while DIF measures differential functioning, making overlap-based labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. Semi-synthetic dose-response confirms detection works when present; the disconnect is in the ground truth.
16	
17	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
18	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
19	
20	\looseness=-1
21	We propose running temporal MH-DIF whenever $\geq 500$ new models accumulate ($N \geq 80$ suffices per \Cref{sec:validation}; 500 ensures family diversity). Empirical $\rho$ degradation simulation---DIF-ordered versus random item removal across six benchmarks---grounds graduated thresholds:
22	(1)~\emph{Monitor} (C\% $< 15\%$): $\Delta\rho < 0.001$ vs.\ random on all benchmarks; report C\% in release notes.
23	(2)~\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\%$): $\Delta\rho = 0.001$--$0.003$ on 4/6 benchmarks; report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings.
24	(3)~\emph{Act} (C\% $\geq 25\%$): WinoGrande drops below $\rho = 0.995$ at 25\% DIF-ordered removal; review flagged item content, cross-reference with contamination indicators, report stable-item rankings as primary comparability metric.
25	Thresholds reflect where DIF-ordered degradation reliably exceeds random removal. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}; temporal C\% complements composite quality indices~\citep{zhu2026bhi}.
26	
```

Regardless of attribution, temporal DIF means the same score carries different meaning across model generations.\n\n\\subsection{Ground Truth Meta-Critique}\n\\label{sec:disc_groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThe near-independence between DIF flags and web-overlap labels (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}) reflects a category error: web-overlap measures global exposure, while DIF measures differential functioning, making overlap-based labels an inappropriate ground truth for a differential method. Semi-synthetic dose-response confirms detection works when present; the disconnect is in the ground truth.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe propose running temporal MH-DIF whenever $\\geq 500$ new models accumulate ($N \\geq 80$ suffices per \\Cref{sec:validation}; 500 ensures family diversity). Empirical $\\rho$ degradation simulation---DIF-ordered versus random item removal across six benchmarks---grounds graduated thresholds:\n(1)~\\emph{Monitor} (C\\% $< 15\\%$): $\\Delta\\rho < 0.001$ vs.\\ random on all benchmarks; report C\\% in release notes.\n(2)~\\emph{Flag} ($15$--$25\\%$): $\\Delta\\rho = 0.001$--$0.003$ on 4/6 benchmarks; report stable-item rankings alongside full rankings.\n(3)~\\emph{Act} (C\\% $\\geq 25\\%$): WinoGrande drops below $\\rho = 0.995$ at 25\\% DIF-ordered removal; review flagged item content, cross-reference with contamination indicators, report stable-item rankings as primary comparability metric.\nThresholds reflect where DIF-ordered degradation reliably exceeds random removal. DIF pre-screening identifies stable anchor items for equating~\\citep{kolen2014equating,habba2026growingpains}; temporal C\\% complements composite quality indices~\\citep{zhu2026bhi}.\n",

DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).",
    "newString": "\\item \\textbf{Temporal measurement instability at scale}: Approximately 31\\% of MMLU items exhibit differential functioning across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher DIF rates than procedural ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. This pattern persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of the DIF signal is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this measurement instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the approximately 31\\% temporal DIF rate cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}). MH-DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts.\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter: DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal), and model families show distinctive DIF profiles (Llama-3 gains ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$).\n\nOur contributions are methodological and empirical, not algorithmic:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique}: Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework}: Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal measurement instability at scale}: Approximately 31\\% of MMLU items exhibit differential functioning across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$ (45--174$\\times$ above random baselines), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher DIF rates than procedural ones. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal) and exhibit a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

Page 8 now shows §5 Discussion (all three subsections) and §6 Conclusion, all fitting within page 8. The last visible text is "monitoring alongside score reporting." which is the end of the Conclusion. Limitations starts on page 9. This is exactly what the Director wanted.

Now I can proceed with Priority B and C edits. Let me read all the files I need for the remaining edits.

Priority B:
- B1: §1 Contribution 2 - add "under single-subject contamination" (introduction.tex)


- B2: Abstract pruning (abstract.tex)
- B3: Table 1 terminology (experiments.tex)
- B4: §4.2 add family-level CI (experiments.tex)
- B5: §3.2 LLM-specific adaptation (method.tex)

Priority C:

- C5: Missing references (references.bib + related_work.tex or method.tex)

Now I'm tracking the page budget impact. B2 saves about half a line by trimming the abstract, while B4 and B5 each add roughly two lines of text. Since B4 sits on pages 4-5 and B5 on pages 3-4, they should fit without pushing the Conclusion off page 8. B3 and B6 are roughly neutral, and C1 adds just a parenthetical.

The key constraint is that B4 and B5 are additive edits in the main body before page 8, so they'll shift content downward. My strategy is to handle the non-additive changes first (B1, B2, B3, B6, C2, C3), compile to check the margin, then tackle the additive edits (B4, B5, C1, C4, C5) once I know how much space I have left.

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested---and existing contamination detectors validate against ground truth that conflates global exposure with differential functioning. We develop a semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching that achieves zero false positive inflation under single-subject contamination ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$; $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.01$ for $k \leq 5$ subjects) while preserving detection power under worst-case uniform injection ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$), and show that web-overlap contamination labels are near-independent of Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) flags (enrichment $\approx 0.96$, $\phi = 0.012$)---exposing a category error in prevailing validation methodology. Applying MH-DIF with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 items), we find 17.9--31.3\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF (ETS~C) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts, each 45--174$\times$ above random baselines, with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks more affected (C\% $\geq 26.9\%$) than procedural ones (C\% $\leq 18.9\%$). This does not threaten aggregate ranking validity ($\rho \approx 0.997$, $\tau \approx 0.958$), but specific model families show concentrated rank shifts (Llama-3: ${+}53$ median positions, Mistral: $-35$) and 2.0\% of pairwise rankings reverse. Item-level decomposition shows 92.4\% of the signal is item-specific; DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8$ to $-49$) with a difficulty--direction interaction ($\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
5	
6	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
7	\label{sec:validation}
8	
9	\looseness=-1
10	As approximate parametric bounds, a Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications per condition) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\bar{b} = -0.84$; 14,042 MMLU items) confirms that at $N \geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$ (details in \Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold. Power varies substantially with item discrimination: TPR ranges from 0.40 at $a = 0.5$ to 0.95 at $a = 2.0$; the gap between TPR at mean $a$ (0.87) and the aggregate TPR (0.68) quantifies the power cost of parameter heterogeneity, consistent with Jensen's inequality (\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).
11	
12	\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\% Measurement Instability}
13	\label{sec:temporal}
14	
15	\looseness=-1
16	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; 95\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\%, 38.1\%], $B = 1{,}000$ model-level resamples; rightward asymmetry reflects heavy-tailed C\% from subjects exceeding 40\%). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\% $\to$ 31.30\%), because the dual ETS threshold already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.
17	
18	\looseness=-1
19	Three controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps~\citep{holland1988mh}. The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ with 76.4\% distributional overlap. Under 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$, confirming at most $\sim$2.4~pp is attributable to ability mismatch. Permutation tests confirm ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
20	
21	\looseness=-1
22	Across all 57 subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\%). Item-level regression shows 92.4\% of the DIF signal is item-specific (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$). A Simpson's paradox confirms that DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate accuracy gains: domain accuracy positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level ($\mathrm{OR} = 1.55$, $p = 0.001$) but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\mathrm{OR} = 0.73$, $p = 0.030$). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($\rho = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while showing relative erosion on easy ones. The conclusion is robust across thresholds ($18.3\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$).
23	
24	\begin{table}[t]
25	\centering
26	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. FPR is the flagging rate on web-overlap ``clean'' items~\citep{li2024contamination}, not a traditional false positive rate. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes indicate comparisons where Li et al.\ labels are not applicable.}
27	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}
28	\small
29	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
30	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
31	\toprule
32	Cohort Definition & $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\% & FPR (clean) & Enrich. \\
33	\midrule
34	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\
35	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,600 / ${\sim}$2,600 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
36	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
37	Random split (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.18 & -- & -- \\
38	Within-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\
39	\bottomrule
40	\end{tabular}}
41	\end{table}
42	
43	\subsection{Ground Truth Mismatch}
44	\label{sec:groundtruth}
45	
46	\looseness=-1
47	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items confirms that the two methods capture orthogonal signals. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\phi = 0.012$, $\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. This near-independence holds across contamination subtypes (input-only enrichment $= 0.986$, input-and-label $= 0.960$). The null result does not indicate detection failure: semi-synthetic injection produces a clear dose-response ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). The disconnect arises because web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training corpora), while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning} (whether an item's relationship to ability changes across cohorts). The Li et al.\ labels are the wrong ground truth for a differential method. 
48	\begin{figure}[t]
49	\centering
50	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
51	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$, confirming that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
52	\label{fig:dose_response}
53	\end{figure}
54	
55	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
56	\label{sec:structural}
57	
58	\looseness=-1
59	Three structural controls fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$), and within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$; range 31.1--45.2\% across 5 representative subjects). The elevated within-subject rate partly reflects local dependence: 11.3\% of within-subject item pairs exceed $|Q3| > 0.20$ vs.\ 2.2\% cross-subject, confirming that cross-subject C\% $= 31.3\%$ is the more conservative estimate. Logistic regression DIF confirms 92.1\% concordance with MH and reveals additional non-uniform DIF (24.8\%), making 31\% conservative. Graduated de-duplication ($K{=}10$: 22.7\%; $K{=}20$: 29.1\%) confirms family structure does not inflate C\%. Ability-matched analysis reduces Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3 C\% from 61.3\% to $28.3\%$ and temporal C\% from 31.3\% to $28.9\%$---consistent matched values ($\sim$28--29\%) despite vastly different unmatched rates, suggesting genuine item-level DIF accounts for $\sim$28--29\% of items. Six alternative explanations were tested; none reduces C\% below $\sim$28\% (\Cref{app:structural}).
60	
61	\begin{table}[t]
62	\centering
63	\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. None reduces temporal C\% below 31\%, indicating that the observed instability is not an artifact of ability matching.}
64	\label{tab:structural_comparison}
65	\small
66	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
67	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
68	\toprule
69	Approach & Mechanism & Temp.\ C\% & TPR ($p{=}0.5$) & Outcome \\
70	\midrule
71	Baseline (ext.) & Cross-bench.\ total score & $0.314_{\pm.009}$ & $0.834_{\pm.027}$ & Reference \\
72	Matched-ability & Within-quintile temp.\ DIF & ${\sim}$0.31 & -- & Enrich.\ 0.94--1.04 \\
73	$\theta$-cond. & $\theta$ replaces total score & 0.312 & 0.876 & $r(\theta,\mathrm{total}) {=} 0.9986$ \\
74	Within-subject & Per-subject DIF & -- & -- & C\% $= 38.1\%$ ($>$25\%) \\
75	\bottomrule
76	\end{tabular}}
77	\end{table}
78	
79	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Framework Validation}
80	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
81	
82	\looseness=-1
83	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$, upper-bound estimate under uniform binary flipping; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average. Multi-subject injection ($k \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 20\}$ subjects, 5 random draws each) confirms robustness: $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$ for $k \leq 5$ ($\leq$9\% of domains); at $k = 10$ (18\%), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069, marking the boundary of matching variable integrity (\Cref{app:method_details,app:fig_matching}).
84	
85	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
86	\label{sec:downstream}
87	
88	\looseness=-1
89	Stable-item rank preservation holds across all six benchmarks (mean $\rho = 0.997$, $\tau = 0.958$; pairwise flip rate $\leq 2.8\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): aggregate ordering is preserved despite substantial measurement instability. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\ random removal; circularity ablation in \Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Model families show distinctive DIF profiles: Llama-3 derivatives ($n = 315$) gain ${+}53$ median rank positions, while Mistral derivatives ($n = 619$) lose $35$ (\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Among the 200 most displaced models, 33.6\% of pairwise rankings reverse---for instance, Qwen1.5-14B-Chat ranks 610 positions ahead of mistral-7b-dpo-v6 on all items but 501 positions behind on stable items alone. Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ larger than random removal; $p < 0.001$), confirming DIF items carry concentrated inter-generational signal. That $\rho \approx 0.997$ coexists with these local reversals illustrates a general pattern: aggregate stability can mask substantial model-level instability.
90	
91	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
92	\label{sec:gsm8k}
93	
94	\looseness=-1
95	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), each 45--174$\times$ above random baselines. Knowledge/reasoning benchmarks (WinoGrande~\citep{sakaguchi2021winogrande}, TruthfulQA~\citep{lin2022truthfulqa}, ARC~\citep{clark2018arc}) show C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ versus $17.9$--$18.9\%$ for procedural ones, consistent with knowledge-intensive items being more sensitive to training data composition shifts.
96

C\% = ETS~C items ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts; $\rho$/$\tau$ = Spearman/Kendall correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip = pairwise rank-flip rate. All six show substantial DIF (45--174$\times$ above random baseline), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks exhibiting higher C\% than procedural ones.}
100	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}

DIF remains substantial even within a 6-month window (2023-H2 vs.\ 2024-H1: MMLU C\% $= 34.35\%$, TruthfulQA $= 31.33\%$).

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAs approximate parametric bounds, a Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications per condition) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$; 14,042 MMLU items) confirms that at $N \\geq 80$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80, FPR of 0.036--0.042, and AUC of 0.93--0.94 for items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$ (details in \\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold. Power varies substantially with item discrimination: TPR ranges from 0.40 at $a = 0.5$ to 0.95 at $a = 2.0$; the gap between TPR at mean $a$ (0.87) and the aggregate TPR (0.68) quantifies the power cost of parameter heterogeneity, consistent with Jensen's inequality (\\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Temporal Comparability: 31\\% Measurement Instability}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 95\\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\\%, 38.1\\%], $B = 1{,}000$ model-level resamples; rightward asymmetry reflects heavy-tailed C\\% from subjects exceeding 40\\%). A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps~\\citep{holland1988mh}. The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ with 76.4\\% distributional overlap. Under 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$, confirming at most $\\sim$2.4~pp is attributable to ability mismatch. Permutation tests confirm ($p < 0.01$; \\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAcross all 57 subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \\emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\\%). Item-level regression shows 92.4\\% of the DIF signal is item-specific (pseudo $R^2 = 0.0007$). A Simpson's paradox confirms that DIF is associated with item properties rather than aggregate accuracy gains: domain accuracy positively predicts DIF at the aggregate level ($\\mathrm{OR} = 1.55$, $p = 0.001$) but reverses after controlling for item difficulty and discrimination ($\\mathrm{OR} = 0.73$, $p = 0.030$). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($\\rho = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while showing relative erosion on easy ones. The conclusion is robust across thresholds ($18.3\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. FPR is the flagging rate on web-overlap ``clean'' items~\\citep{li2024contamination}, not a traditional false positive rate. The enrichment ratio is 0.964 (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Association tests yield $\\phi = 0.012$, $\\chi^2 = 1.28$ ($p = 0.258$), and OR $= 1.05$ [0.96, 1.15]; the Jaccard index between flagged sets is 0.206. Six alternative explanations were tested; none reduces C\\% below $\\sim$28\\% (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Structural approaches to reducing temporal DIF. DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal; circularity ablation in \\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). ```text
30	The Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \leq |\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\%. For example, if 2024 models consistently outperform 2023 models on a formal-logic item \emph{after controlling for overall ability}, the pooled odds ratio $\alpha_{\mathrm{MH}} = 0.42$ yields $\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \ln(0.42) = 2.04$, classified as ETS~C ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.001$).
31	
32	The choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\emph{cross-subject matching}, adapting purified subtest matching~\citep{swaminathan1990logistic} to the LLM multi-subject setting, uses the total score on all subjects \emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\theta$ correlates at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores (\Cref{sec:structural}).
33	
34	\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces assumptions absent in educational testing. First, models trained on overlapping corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1,000 iterations $\times$ 8 subjects, Q3 $= 4.6$--$34.2\%$) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$ pp under cross-subject matching with ETS~C (\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants and merges within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\% (\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, the ability distribution is non-normal (bimodal from base vs.\ instruction-tuned models), motivating quintile-based rather than parametric stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57 subjects span multiple latent dimensions, while MH assumes unidimensionality; cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\Cref{sec:structural}). MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not assume a specific IRT model.
35	
36	\subsection{Cohort Designs}
37	\label{sec:cohorts}
38	
39	We construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window, testing whether ability differences alone produce DIF; (3)~\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.
40	
41	\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}
42	\label{sec:injection}
43	
44	To evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.
45	
46	\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \in \{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = \mathrm{TPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{TPR}(\text{baseline})$ and $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = \mathrm{FPR}(\text{injected}) - \mathrm{FPR}(\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation. Because binary response flipping represents a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern, reported $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ values constitute upper-bound estimates of detection sensitivity under more realistic contamination scenarios.
47	
48	\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \emph{other than} the one being tested.
49	
50	\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}
51	\label{prop:fpr}
52	Let $M_i^{(-s)} = \sum_{j \notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.
53	
54	When contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\leq$9\% of subjects ($k \leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).
55	
56	Empirically, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$). $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ is a constructive guarantee of the matching strategy, independent of injection realism, whereas $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ constitutes an upper-bound estimate. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol.
57

The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. For example, if 2024 models consistently outperform 2023 models on a formal-logic item \\emph{after controlling for overall ability}, the pooled odds ratio $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 0.42$ yields $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(0.42) = 2.04$, classified as ETS~C ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.001$).\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching}, adapting purified subtest matching~\\citep{swaminathan1990logistic} to the LLM multi-subject setting, uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\\theta$ correlates at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores (\\Cref{sec:structural}).\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces assumptions absent in educational testing. First, models trained on overlapping corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1,000 iterations $\\times$ 8 subjects, Q3 $= 4.6$--$34.2\\%$) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$ pp under cross-subject matching with ETS~C (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants and merges within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\\% (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, the ability distribution is non-normal (bimodal from base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models), motivating quintile-based rather than parametric stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57 subjects span multiple latent dimensions, while MH assumes unidimensionality; cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\\Cref{sec:structural}). MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not assume a specific IRT model.\n\n\\subsection{Cohort Designs}\n\\label{sec:cohorts}\n\nWe construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window, testing whether ability differences alone produce DIF; (3)~\\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nTo evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.\n\n\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation. Because binary response flipping represents a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern, reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values constitute upper-bound estimates of detection sensitivity under more realistic contamination scenarios.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested.\n\n\\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.\n\nWhen contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\\leq$9\\% of subjects ($k \\leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\nEmpirically, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$). $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ is a constructive guarantee of the matching strategy, independent of injection realism, whereas $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ constitutes an upper-bound estimate. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol.\n",

    }

22	\item \textbf{Ground truth meta-critique}: Web-overlap contamination labels~\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap (\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011SoiVjagwfuBDrAxURgcFo`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{Methodological scope.}
4	MH-DIF detects only uniform DIF; logistic regression reveals an additional 24.8\% non-uniform DIF, making 31\% conservative. More fundamentally, DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently but cannot determine \emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts. A definitive causal decomposition remains out of reach without access to model training data. The semi-synthetic framework uses binary response flipping (incorrect $\to$ correct) to simulate differential contamination, representing a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern. Real-world contamination involves partial memorization, reasoning shortcuts, and format-specific advantages; detection power under such realistic patterns is untested. The moderate $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$ reflects sensitivity to severe differential behavior; the framework's primary methodological contribution---cross-subject matching achieving $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$---is a matching strategy property independent of injection realism.
5	
6	\paragraph{Examinee non-independence.}
7	The MH $\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees, yet LLMs sharing base models, training data, or architectures violate this assumption. Empirical mitigations partially address this: permutation simulation bounds LD-attributable FPR inflation to ${<}0.001$~pp, and graduated de-duplication ($K{=}20$: C\% $= 29.1\%$) shows family structure does not inflate results. \citet{french2010hierarchical} show that MH maintains nominal size under moderate clustering in educational settings, consistent with our empirical findings; however, the extreme relatedness of some LLM families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in that work. Cluster bootstrap resampling at the family level (29 families with $\geq$3 members, 1{,}882 models, $B = 1{,}000$) yields a 95\% CI of $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$, $3.1\times$ wider than the model-level CI $[29.4\%, 38.1\%]$, partly because LLM families are cohort-specific (no family spans both 2023 and 2024), so resampling families simultaneously varies the reference/focal composition. Despite this conservative bound, the lower bound far exceeds the random-split baseline (0.18\%), confirming robustness to within-family dependence.
8	
9	\paragraph{Matching boundary and sample size.}
10	Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; when contamination spans ${>}10$ subjects (${>}18\%$ of MMLU), $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.026--0.069. Model-level bootstrap may underestimate CI width due to within-family correlation; family-level cluster bootstrap (preceding paragraph) confirms robustness despite a $3.1\times$ wider CI. The GSM1K correlation analysis ($N = 33$) has limited statistical power and is exploratory.
11	
12	\paragraph{Generalizability.}
13	All six benchmarks use multiple-choice or binary scoring; generalizability to open-ended generation (e.g., AlpacaEval), coding (e.g., HumanEval), or partial-credit benchmarks is untested and would require polytomous DIF methods. Temporal cohorts are defined by calendar year, a coarse grouping; finer-grained definitions (e.g., by training data cutoff or alignment method) might reveal more targeted patterns.
14	
```

I'm checking whether the B5 addition about cross-subject matching differs from what's already in §3.2—it does, since B5 frames it as a methodological contribution specific to how standard purified MH differs from this approach, rather than just describing the technique itself. For C1, I need to look at what "Among the 200 most displaced models" is being clarified to, which appears to be adding context about the top 3 something.

Now I'm verifying the percentage calculation for those 200 models out of the full sample, and checking what the flip rate statistic represents. For C3, this goes at the end of the Generalizability section. For C4, I'm looking at the Table 3 caption and need to understand what metrics are being reported—the C% for ETS items with certain DIF thresholds, correlation measures between ranking methods, and flip rates.

I'm considering how to integrate the Ratio metric into the caption, specifically clarifying that it's the C% divided by the baseline C%, with the baseline varying across benchmarks. I'll add this definition right after the flip rate explanation.

For C5, I need to verify those two references exist in the bibliography.

    "newString": "achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ under single-subject contamination while preserving detection power",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We apply Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (MH-DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench},\\footnote{Analysis code and DIF audit scripts are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/dif-audit}.} and find that 31\\% of MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$), indicating large shifts in item functioning between models released in 2023 and 2024. This is not statistical noise: a random-split control within the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\\% ETS~C items. This pattern persists across all 57 MMLU subjects (C\\% range 21--48\\%, unweighted mean $\\approx 30\\%$), and item-level regression shows that 92.4\\% of the DIF signal is unrelated to aggregate domain-level accuracy gains. Nearly one in three MMLU items behaves differently depending on when the evaluated models were trained. Crucially, this measurement instability does not threaten benchmark validity: Spearman rank correlation between model rankings computed on all items versus stable-only items is $\\rho \\approx 0.997$. The instability is diagnostic, not destructive; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations rather than rendering MMLU scores unreliable.\n\nA growing body of contamination detection research has produced sophisticated item-level methods, from membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} to performance-based detectors~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}. These methods validate detection accuracy primarily against web-overlap labels such as those of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify benchmark items as contaminated based on verbatim or near-verbatim matches in publicly accessible training corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure}: whether an item exists in the data ecosystem that all training pipelines can access. They do not measure \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts that may have had varying degrees of exposure or different training methodologies. \\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential} illustrates the distinction. Most MMLU items are globally contaminated in the sense that they exist in web-accessible text, so all model cohorts encounter them and no differential signal arises. The items that function differentially are those where exposure or training methodology changed asymmetrically across cohorts. Validating differential detectors against global-exposure labels therefore conflates two phenomena. Direct comparison confirms near-independence between the two flag sets (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), consistent across contamination subtypes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination, illustrated via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts because only that cohort was disproportionately exposed during training. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most MMLU items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe apply classical MH-DIF with ETS classification to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge, the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks. This psychometric approach complements concurrent work on anchor-item calibration for score comparability~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and addresses the fragility of existing contamination detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Semi-synthetic injection experiments confirm that MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at exploitation rate $p = 0.5$), and our cross-subject matching protocol eliminates false positive inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$). Three structural controls (matched-ability quintile analysis, IRT $\\theta$-conditioning, and within-subject pre-checks) confirm that the approximately 31\\% temporal DIF rate cannot be attributed to methodological artifacts, establishing it as a robust empirical finding about LLM benchmark measurement properties, replicated across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:gsm8k}). MH-DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}---the signal may reflect contamination, capability change, or training methodology shifts.\n\nMMLU's 12,508 items provide sufficient measurement redundancy that removing nearly a third does not alter rank order ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$). DIF should therefore be understood as a diagnostic lens rather than a quality filter: DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal ($z = -8$ to $-49$ vs.\\ random removal), and model families show distinctive DIF profiles (Llama-3 gains ${+}53$ median rank positions, Mistral loses $35$).\n\nOur contributions are methodological and empirical, not algorithmic:\n\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Ground truth meta-critique}: Web-overlap contamination labels~\\citep{li2024contamination} do not provide appropriate ground truth for item-level differential detectors. Direct empirical comparison on 9,229 items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$), exposing a validation methodology gap (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation framework}: Controlled injection with cross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$); cross-benchmark replication on GSM8K confirms generality (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic,sec:gsm8k}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal measurement instability at scale}: Approximately 31\\% of MMLU items exhibit differential functioning across model generations under ETS~C classification; replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks yields C\\% $= 17.9$--$31.3\\%$, with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks showing higher DIF rates than procedural ones (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:gsm8k,sec:downstream}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested---and existing contamination detectors validate against ground truth that conflates global exposure with differential functioning. We develop a semi-synthetic injection framework with cross-subject matching that achieves zero false positive inflation under single-subject contamination ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$; $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.01$ for $k \\leq 5$ subjects) while preserving detection power under worst-case uniform injection ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$), and show that web-overlap contamination labels are near-independent of Mantel-Haenszel Differential Item Functioning (DIF) flags (enrichment $\\approx 0.96$, $\\phi = 0.012$)---exposing a category error in prevailing validation methodology. Applying MH-DIF with ETS severity classification to six METABENCH benchmarks (5,227 models, 27,125 items), we find 17.9--31.3\\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF (ETS~C) between 2023 and 2024 cohorts, each 45--174$\\times$ above random baselines, with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks more affected (C\\% $\\geq 26.9\\%$) than procedural ones (C\\% $\\leq 18.9\\%$). This does not threaten aggregate ranking validity ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$, $\\tau \\approx 0.958$), but specific model families show concentrated rank shifts (Llama-3: ${+}53$ median positions, Mistral: $-35$) and 2.0\\% of pairwise rankings reverse. Item-level decomposition shows 92.4\\% of the signal is item-specific; DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative information ($z = -8$ to $-49$) with a difficulty--direction interaction ($\\rho = -0.597$): gains on hard items, erosion on easy ones. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

14	
15	\looseness=-1
16	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; 95\% bootstrap CI: [29.4\%, 38.1\%], $B = 1{,}000$ model-level resamples; rightward asymmetry reflects heavy-tailed C\% from subjects exceeding 40\%). Benjamini-Hochberg FDR correction at $q = 0.05$ removes only 3 items (31.32\% $\to$ 31.30\%), because the dual ETS threshold already provides implicit multiple-testing protection.
17	
18	\looseness=-1
19	Three controls confirm this reflects genuine temporal change. A random split of the 2023 cohort yields only 0.18\% ETS~C items (sampling noise), and an ability-quintile comparison within the same temporal cohort yields 2.9\% (ability differences alone). A within-family comparison (Llama-2 vs.\ Llama-3) produces 61.3\% ETS~C items, elevated by large ability gaps~\citep{holland1988mh}. The temporal cohort ability gap is negligible: Cohen's $d = -0.17$ with 76.4\% distributional overlap. Under 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N = 638$ per group, 5 seeds), C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$, confirming at most $\sim$2.4~pp is attributable to ability mismatch. Permutation tests confirm ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "584e0562-e4a4-4c6d-983b-c0d933874727",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "84f9810e-5108-4b9e-81bc-da76e752c07c",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "3419aa9e-b158-46dd-9427-34ed1ae7b695",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/method.tex",
    "oldString": "MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not assume a specific IRT model.",
    "newString": "MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not assume a specific IRT model. Unlike standard purified MH that conditions on total test score, we match on cross-subject total score to avoid criterion contamination when target-subject items themselves may be compromised---an adaptation specific to multi-domain LLM benchmarks where subjects may be differentially affected.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.\n\n\\subsection{Data}\n\\label{sec:data}\n\nThe primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.\n\n\\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}\n\\label{sec:mhdif}\n\nIn educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.\n\nThe Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\\citep{holland1988mh} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}. Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \\times 2$ contingency table:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mh}\n\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = \\frac{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \\,/\\, T_k}{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \\,/\\, T_k}\\,,\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:delta}\n\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}})\\,.\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. For example, if 2024 models consistently outperform 2023 models on a formal-logic item \\emph{after controlling for overall ability}, the pooled odds ratio $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 0.42$ yields $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(0.42) = 2.04$, classified as ETS~C ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.001$).\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching}, adapting purified subtest matching~\\citep{swaminathan1990logistic} to the LLM multi-subject setting, uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on GSM8K items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is sufficient: IRT $\\theta$ correlates at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores (\\Cref{sec:structural}).\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces assumptions absent in educational testing. First, models trained on overlapping corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1,000 iterations $\\times$ 8 subjects, Q3 $= 4.6$--$34.2\\%$) confirms LD-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$ pp under cross-subject matching with ETS~C (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Second, fine-tuned variants and merges within model families are not independent examinees; graduated de-duplication shows family structure does not inflate C\\% (\\Cref{sec:structural}). Third, the ability distribution is non-normal (bimodal from base vs.\\ instruction-tuned models), motivating quintile-based rather than parametric stratification. Fourth, MMLU's 57 subjects span multiple latent dimensions, while MH assumes unidimensionality; cross-subject matching and within-subject DIF checks address this (\\Cref{sec:structural}). MH-DIF is non-parametric and does not assume a specific IRT model.\n\n\\subsection{Cohort Designs}\n\\label{sec:cohorts}\n\nWe construct five cohort comparisons: (1)~\\emph{Temporal}: 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) vs.\\ 2024 ($N = 807$) models, the primary comparison; (2)~\\emph{Ability-quintile}: Q1--Q2 vs.\\ Q4--Q5 within the same temporal window, testing whether ability differences alone produce DIF; (3)~\\emph{Within-family}: Llama-2 ($N = 161$) vs.\\ Llama-3 ($N = 308$), isolating generational changes within a single family; (4)~\\emph{Within-subject}: per-subject DIF to test domain concentration; and (5)~\\emph{Random split}: two random halves of the 2023 cohort ($N = 540$ each) as a sanity check.\n\n\\subsection{Semi-Synthetic Injection Framework}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nTo evaluate whether MH-DIF can detect differential contamination when it is present, we construct a semi-synthetic framework with controlled ground truth.\n\n\\paragraph{Procedure.} From items classified as ETS~A in the uninjected data (negligible DIF), we select 20\\% uniformly at random as the ``contaminated'' set. For each contaminated item, we flip incorrect responses to correct in the focal group at exploitation rate $p \\in \\{0.0, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$. We then run MH-DIF on the modified response matrix and measure detection performance. We report $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{TPR}(\\text{baseline})$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{injected}) - \\mathrm{FPR}(\\text{baseline})$, where TPR is the true positive rate on injected items and FPR is the false positive rate on uninjected items. Each injection level is repeated across 5 random seeds for uncertainty estimation. Because binary response flipping represents a worst-case uniform exploitation pattern, reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values constitute upper-bound estimates of detection sensitivity under more realistic contamination scenarios.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-subject matching.} The choice of matching variable is critical for valid semi-synthetic evaluation. When contamination is injected into items within subject $X$, the total score on subject $X$ absorbs part of the injection signal. Using this contaminated total score as the MH matching variable violates the conditional independence assumption and inflates FPR. Cross-subject matching resolves this by computing the matching variable from all subjects \\emph{other than} the one being tested.\n\n\\paragraph{Remark (Zero FPR under single-subject matching).}\n\\label{prop:fpr}\nLet $M_i^{(-s)} = \\sum_{j \\notin s} Y_{ij}$ be the matching variable for examinee $i$ on focal subject $s$. Under single-subject injection (contamination confined to subject $s$), the tested item belongs to $s$ and does not contribute to $M_i^{(-s)}$; injecting contamination into $s$ leaves $M_i^{(-s)}$ unchanged. Therefore $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ by construction.\n\nWhen contamination spans multiple subjects simultaneously, $M_i^{(-s)}$ may absorb signal from other contaminated subjects, potentially inflating FPR. Empirically, this guarantee extends to multi-domain contamination affecting $\\leq$9\\% of subjects ($k \\leq 5$), with graceful degradation beyond this boundary (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\nEmpirically, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU domains (bootstrap 95\\% CI $= [0.000, 0.000]$). $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ is a constructive guarantee of the matching strategy, independent of injection realism, whereas $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ constitutes an upper-bound estimate. We adopt cross-subject matching as the standard protocol.\n\nDiagnostic procedures (Yen's Q3 local independence, domain-specificity analysis, DIF decomposition) and statistical details (multiple testing correction, threshold sensitivity, architecture heterogeneity checks) appear in \\Cref{app:method_details}.\n",
